Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.

But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.

But yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.

But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.

Spiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.

So, I don’t know. Maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I haven’t really had that same “oh crap, I need to get out of here” thing unless it’s the bat stuff.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… it’s kind of embarrassing, but what brings me in is this thing that happens at work. I work retail, and sometimes if I even think there might be a bat, or like something flapping around back in the stockroom, I just… my heart jumps right away. I start sweating, and all I can think is I need to get out of there right then.\n\nIt’s weird because the stockroom’s already kind of a mess, boxes everywhere, and people leave the back door open sometimes—anyway, I know that’s not really the point, but yeah, that reaction is why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bats that trigger this reaction, or are there other things that can cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah… or things that seem like they could be one. Like, I know in my head a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something dark move fast or there’s a quick flutter down the back hallway, my brain just goes, “danger,” right away.\n\nIt doesn’t even have to be an actual bat, I guess. Sometimes it’s just that shape or that movement. The stockroom lights are kind of awful too, they flicker sometimes, which probably doesn’t help… but yeah, it’s mainly anything that looks or moves like a bat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats or things that might be bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really in the same way. Outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself walking around anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety when you're not in a situation where you might encounter a bat or something that resembles one, such as when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: At home? Yeah, maybe. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.

It’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.

A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.

And the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. I tell people it’s just because I don’t feel like dealing with the carts back there or whatever, but really I’m thinking about bats.

If I have to check the stairwell, I still do it, but I kind of rush through it. I’ll have my phone in my hand with the flashlight ready, and I’m tense the whole time, like I’m waiting for something to fly at me. It’s embarrassing, honestly.

I start sweating, and all I can think is I need to get out of there right then.\n\nIt’s weird because the stockroom’s already kind of a mess, boxes everywhere, and people leave the back door open sometimes—anyway, I know that’s not really the point, but yeah, that reaction is why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bats that trigger this reaction, or are there other things that can cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah… or things that seem like they could be one. Sometimes it’s just that shape or that movement. The stockroom lights are kind of awful too, they flicker sometimes, which probably doesn’t help… but yeah, it’s mainly anything that looks or moves like a bat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats or things that might be bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really in the same way. Outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself walking around anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety when you're not in a situation where you might encounter a bat or something that resembles one, such as when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: At home? Um… I don’t think so, not really. Like, I can be annoyed or stressed about regular stuff—bills, dishes piling up, my neighbor’s dog barking at weird hours, that kind of thing—but I wouldn’t call it the same anxiety.\n\nWith friends, I’m usually okay too. Unless someone starts joking about bats, I guess, or tells some story and my mind goes there. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… it’s kind of embarrassing, but what brings me in is this thing that happens at work. I work retail, and sometimes if I even think there might be a bat, or like something flapping around back in the stockroom, I just… my heart jumps right away. I start sweating, and all I can think is I need to get out of there right then.\n\nIt’s weird because the stockroom’s already kind of a mess, boxes everywhere, and people leave the back door open sometimes—anyway, I know that’s not really the point, but yeah, that reaction is why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bats that trigger this reaction, or are there other things that can cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah… or things that seem like they could be one. Like, I know in my head a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something dark move fast or there’s a quick flutter down the back hallway, my brain just goes, “danger,” right away.\n\nIt doesn’t even have to be an actual bat, I guess. Sometimes it’s just that shape or that movement. The stockroom lights are kind of awful too, they flicker sometimes, which probably doesn’t help… but yeah, it’s mainly anything that looks or moves like a bat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats or things that might be bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really in the same way. I mean, work can be stressful, sure—customers get mad about coupons or returns, and then the register freezes, and my manager’s asking where some shipment went, you know how it is. But that feels like normal stress, I guess.\n\nThe on-edge feeling I came in about is more tied to that bat thing, or thinking something might be one. Outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself walking around anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety when you're not in a situation where you might encounter a bat or something that resembles one, such as when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: At home? Um… I don’t think so, not really. Like, I can be annoyed or stressed about regular stuff—bills, dishes piling up, my neighbor’s dog barking at weird hours, that kind of thing—but I wouldn’t call it the same anxiety.\n\nWith friends, I’m usually okay too. Unless someone starts joking about bats, I guess, or tells some story and my mind goes there. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t usually sit there at home thinking, “what if I panic,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after a long shift I’m kind of wound up, but that’s more like… my feet hurt, I’m replaying some annoying customer thing in my head, or wondering if I forgot to put something back on the shelf. But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? Like if I know we’re short-staffed or there’s a sale and people are going to be lined up complaining about prices not ringing right.\n\nBut anxious like that? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I haven’t really had that same “oh crap, I need to get out of here” thing unless it’s the bat stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be triggered by something that isn't a bat but has some similarity to it, such as a bird or another flying creature?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.\n\nAnd the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.\n\nAt home it’s not really the same setup, you know? But the main avoiding is definitely those work areas.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides bats or things that resemble bats, that can trigger a similar anxious reaction in you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, nothing really comes to mind the way the bat thing does. Like, I get stressed about normal stuff—customers being rude, schedules changing, my car making that noise again, which I keep meaning to get looked at—but that’s not the same kind of jolt.\n\nSo… besides bats or something that my brain thinks might be a bat, I haven’t really noticed anything else that sets me off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.\n\nAnd the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. I tell people it’s just because I don’t feel like dealing with the carts back there or whatever, but really I’m thinking about bats.\n\nIf I have to check the stairwell, I still do it, but I kind of rush through it. I’ll have my phone in my hand with the flashlight ready, and I’m tense the whole time, like I’m waiting for something to fly at me. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.\n\nAt home it’s not really the same setup, you know? Though now that you ask, I’m thinking about my garage light, it flickers sometimes and I really need to change the bulb, but I keep forgetting—well, maybe not forgetting, I just hate messing with it. But the main avoiding is definitely those work areas.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Social life… I don’t know, I’m not exactly out camping every weekend or anything. I mostly just go home, watch TV, maybe grab food with a buddy if schedules line up. My brother keeps trying to get me to go to this outdoor movie thing, but honestly that’s not really the big issue.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… it’s kind of embarrassing, but what brings me in is this thing that happens at work. I work retail, and sometimes if I even think there might be a bat, or like something flapping around back in the stockroom, I just… my heart jumps right away. I start sweating, and all I can think is I need to get out of there right then.\n\nIt’s weird because the stockroom’s already kind of a mess, boxes everywhere, and people leave the back door open sometimes—anyway, I know that’s not really the point, but yeah, that reaction is why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bats that trigger this reaction, or are there other things that can cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah… or things that seem like they could be one. Like, I know in my head a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something dark move fast or there’s a quick flutter down the back hallway, my brain just goes, “danger,” right away.\n\nIt doesn’t even have to be an actual bat, I guess. Sometimes it’s just that shape or that movement. The stockroom lights are kind of awful too, they flicker sometimes, which probably doesn’t help… but yeah, it’s mainly anything that looks or moves like a bat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats or things that might be bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really in the same way. Outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself walking around anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety when you're not in a situation where you might encounter a bat or something that resembles one, such as when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: At home? Um… I don’t think so, not really. Like, I can be annoyed or stressed about regular stuff—bills, dishes piling up, my neighbor’s dog barking at weird hours, that kind of thing—but I wouldn’t call it the same anxiety.\n\nWith friends, I’m usually okay too. Unless someone starts joking about bats, I guess, or tells some story and my mind goes there. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t usually sit there at home thinking, “what if I panic,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after a long shift I’m kind of wound up, but that’s more like… my feet hurt, I’m replaying some annoying customer thing in my head, or wondering if I forgot to put something back on the shelf. But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? Like if I know we’re short-staffed or there’s a sale and people are going to be lined up complaining about prices not ringing right.\n\nBut anxious like that? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t usually sit there at home thinking, “what if I panic,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after a long shift I’m kind of wound up, but that’s more like… my feet hurt, I’m replaying some annoying customer thing in my head, or wondering if I forgot to put something back on the shelf. But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? Like if I know we’re short-staffed or there’s a sale and people are going to be lined up complaining about prices not ringing right.\n\nBut anxious like that? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I haven’t really had that same “oh crap, I need to get out of here” thing unless it’s the bat stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be triggered by something that isn't a bat but has some similarity to it, such as a bird or another flying creature?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides bats or things that resemble bats, that can trigger a similar anxious reaction in you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, nothing really comes to mind the way the bat thing does. Like, I get stressed about normal stuff—customers being rude, schedules changing, my car making that noise again, which I keep meaning to get looked at—but that’s not the same kind of jolt.\n\nSo… besides bats or something that my brain thinks might be a bat, I haven’t really noticed anything else that sets me off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.\n\nAnd the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. I tell people it’s just because I don’t feel like dealing with the carts back there or whatever, but really I’m thinking about bats.\n\nIf I have to check the stairwell, I still do it, but I kind of rush through it. I’ll have my phone in my hand with the flashlight ready, and I’m tense the whole time, like I’m waiting for something to fly at me. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.\n\nAt home it’s not really the same setup, you know? Though now that you ask, I’m thinking about my garage light, it flickers sometimes and I really need to change the bulb, but I keep forgetting—well, maybe not forgetting, I just hate messing with it. But the main avoiding is definitely those work areas.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your social life or leisure activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Social life… I don’t know, I’m not exactly out camping every weekend or anything. I mostly just go home, watch TV, maybe grab food with a buddy if schedules line up. My brother keeps trying to get me to go to this outdoor movie thing, but honestly that’s not really the big issue.\n\nThe bigger thing is work. Like, the closing stuff has become a problem. My manager’s started kind of questioning whether I can really handle being shift lead because I keep trading out of closing duties and I’m not dependable for those after-hours checks. And I was trying to work toward a permanent management position, so… yeah, it feels like this bat thing could actually mess that up. That’s probably what scares me most besides the bat itself, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

I don’t think so, not the same way. Like, if it was a bird or something in the back hallway, I’d probably jump because nobody likes something suddenly flying around near their head, you know? But with bats it’s different.

My manager’s started kind of questioning whether I can really handle being shift lead because I keep trading out of closing duties and I’m not dependable for those after-hours checks. That’s probably what scares me most besides the bat itself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar reaction if you encountered another animal that you're not typically afraid of, in the same situation where you usually fear bats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not the same way. A big moth… I mean, I’d probably hate it, yeah. Nobody wants some thing flapping around in a stockroom, especially with those awful lights back there—half the time they buzz anyway, which doesn’t help.

Like, I can be annoyed or stressed about regular stuff—bills, dishes piling up, my neighbor’s dog barking at weird hours, that kind of thing—but I wouldn’t call it the same anxiety.\n\nWith friends, I’m usually okay too. Unless someone starts joking about bats, I guess, or tells some story and my mind goes there. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t usually sit there at home thinking, “what if I panic,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after a long shift I’m kind of wound up, but that’s more like… my feet hurt, I’m replaying some annoying customer thing in my head, or wondering if I forgot to put something back on the shelf. But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? Like if I know we’re short-staffed or there’s a sale and people are going to be lined up complaining about prices not ringing right.\n\nBut anxious like that? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I haven’t really had that same “oh crap, I need to get out of here” thing unless it’s the bat stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be triggered by something that isn't a bat but has some similarity to it, such as a bird or another flying creature?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides bats or things that resemble bats, that can trigger a similar anxious reaction in you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, nothing really comes to mind the way the bat thing does. Like, I get stressed about normal stuff—customers being rude, schedules changing, my car making that noise again, which I keep meaning to get looked at—but that’s not the same kind of jolt.\n\nSo… besides bats or something that my brain thinks might be a bat, I haven’t really noticed anything else that sets me off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.\n\nAnd the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. I tell people it’s just because I don’t feel like dealing with the carts back there or whatever, but really I’m thinking about bats.\n\nIf I have to check the stairwell, I still do it, but I kind of rush through it. I’ll have my phone in my hand with the flashlight ready, and I’m tense the whole time, like I’m waiting for something to fly at me. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.\n\nAt home it’s not really the same setup, you know? Though now that you ask, I’m thinking about my garage light, it flickers sometimes and I really need to change the bulb, but I keep forgetting—well, maybe not forgetting, I just hate messing with it. But the main avoiding is definitely those work areas.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your social life or leisure activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Social life… I don’t know, I’m not exactly out camping every weekend or anything. I mostly just go home, watch TV, maybe grab food with a buddy if schedules line up. My brother keeps trying to get me to go to this outdoor movie thing, but honestly that’s not really the big issue.\n\nThe bigger thing is work. Like, the closing stuff has become a problem. My manager’s started kind of questioning whether I can really handle being shift lead because I keep trading out of closing duties and I’m not dependable for those after-hours checks. And I was trying to work toward a permanent management position, so… yeah, it feels like this bat thing could actually mess that up. That’s probably what scares me most besides the bat itself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar reaction if you encountered another animal that you're not typically afraid of, in the same situation where you usually fear bats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not the same way. Like, if it was a bird or something in the back hallway, I’d probably jump because nobody likes something suddenly flying around near their head, you know? But with bats it’s different.\n\nI know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I’ve been told that, and I get it when I’m standing here talking about it. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the corner of my eye back there, my brain just goes straight to danger, like I’m in real trouble. It doesn’t really give me time to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you saw a large moth or another flying insect in the stockroom?\n\nPatient: A big moth… I mean, I’d probably hate it, yeah. Nobody wants some thing flapping around in a stockroom, especially with those awful lights back there—half the time they buzz anyway, which doesn’t help.\n\nBut no, I don’t think it would hit me the same as a bat. With bats, even if I know in my head it probably isn’t going to hurt me, the second I see something that looks like one—or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. Like it skips over the thinking part.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

That’s probably what scares me most besides the bat itself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar reaction if you encountered another animal that you're not typically afraid of, in the same situation where you usually fear bats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not the same way. Like, if it was a bird or something in the back hallway, I’d probably jump because nobody likes something suddenly flying around near their head, you know? But with bats it’s different.\n\nI know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I’ve been told that, and I get it when I’m standing here talking about it. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the corner of my eye back there, my brain just goes straight to danger, like I’m in real trouble. It doesn’t really give me time to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you saw a large moth or another flying insect in the stockroom?\n\nPatient: A big moth… I mean, I’d probably hate it, yeah. Nobody wants some thing flapping around in a stockroom, especially with those awful lights back there—half the time they buzz anyway, which doesn’t help.\n\nBut no, I don’t think it would hit me the same as a bat. With bats, even if I know in my head it probably isn’t going to hurt me, the second I see something that looks like one—or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. Like it skips over the thinking part.\n\n[Last Question]\nWould you say your fear is exclusively of bats or do you think you could be equally afraid of other animals if you encountered them in a similar situation?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 37 / retail_salesperson / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Specific_Phobic_Fear\nmanifestation: \"I know a bat in the store probably isn't going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one or even a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind treats it like I'm in real danger.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I’d say it’s mostly bats. I mean, I don’t love animals loose in a store in general—one time we had a pigeon get near the front doors and everyone made a whole production out of it, customers taking videos and everything—but that didn’t do the same thing to me.

With bats, it’s like… I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that right now and it makes sense. But if I see anything that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind treats it like I’m actually in danger. It just hits different than other animals.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n17\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.228, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… it’s kind of embarrassing, but what brings me in is this thing that happens at work. I work retail, and sometimes if I even think there might be a bat, or like something flapping around back in the stockroom, I just… my heart jumps right away. I start sweating, and all I can think is I need to get out of there right then.\n\nIt’s weird because the stockroom’s already kind of a mess, boxes everywhere, and people leave the back door open sometimes—anyway, I know that’s not really the point, but yeah, that reaction is why I’m here.\n\nDoctor: Is it just bats that trigger this reaction, or are there other things that can cause a similar response?\n\nPatient: Mostly bats, yeah… or things that seem like they could be one. Like, I know in my head a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see something dark move fast or there’s a quick flutter down the back hallway, my brain just goes, “danger,” right away.\n\nIt doesn’t even have to be an actual bat, I guess. Sometimes it’s just that shape or that movement. The stockroom lights are kind of awful too, they flicker sometimes, which probably doesn’t help… but yeah, it’s mainly anything that looks or moves like a bat.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel anxious or on edge in situations that have nothing to do with bats or things that might be bats?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t know, not really in the same way. I mean, work can be stressful, sure—customers get mad about coupons or returns, and then the register freezes, and my manager’s asking where some shipment went, you know how it is. But that feels like normal stress, I guess.\n\nThe on-edge feeling I came in about is more tied to that bat thing, or thinking something might be one. Outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself walking around anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety when you're not in a situation where you might encounter a bat or something that resembles one, such as when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: At home? Um… I don’t think so, not really. Like, I can be annoyed or stressed about regular stuff—bills, dishes piling up, my neighbor’s dog barking at weird hours, that kind of thing—but I wouldn’t call it the same anxiety.\n\nWith friends, I’m usually okay too. Unless someone starts joking about bats, I guess, or tells some story and my mind goes there. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t usually sit there at home thinking, “what if I panic,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after a long shift I’m kind of wound up, but that’s more like… my feet hurt, I’m replaying some annoying customer thing in my head, or wondering if I forgot to put something back on the shelf. But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? Like if I know we’re short-staffed or there’s a sale and people are going to be lined up complaining about prices not ringing right.\n\nBut anxious like that? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I haven’t really had that same “oh crap, I need to get out of here” thing unless it’s the bat stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be triggered by something that isn't a bat but has some similarity to it, such as a bird or another flying creature?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides bats or things that resemble bats, that can trigger a similar anxious reaction in you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, nothing really comes to mind the way the bat thing does. Like, I get stressed about normal stuff—customers being rude, schedules changing, my car making that noise again, which I keep meaning to get looked at—but that’s not the same kind of jolt.\n\nSo… besides bats or something that my brain thinks might be a bat, I haven’t really noticed anything else that sets me off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.\n\nAnd the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. I tell people it’s just because I don’t feel like dealing with the carts back there or whatever, but really I’m thinking about bats.\n\nIf I have to check the stairwell, I still do it, but I kind of rush through it. I’ll have my phone in my hand with the flashlight ready, and I’m tense the whole time, like I’m waiting for something to fly at me. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.\n\nAt home it’s not really the same setup, you know? Though now that you ask, I’m thinking about my garage light, it flickers sometimes and I really need to change the bulb, but I keep forgetting—well, maybe not forgetting, I just hate messing with it. But the main avoiding is definitely those work areas.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your social life or leisure activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Social life… I don’t know, I’m not exactly out camping every weekend or anything. I mostly just go home, watch TV, maybe grab food with a buddy if schedules line up. My brother keeps trying to get me to go to this outdoor movie thing, but honestly that’s not really the big issue.\n\nThe bigger thing is work. Like, the closing stuff has become a problem. My manager’s started kind of questioning whether I can really handle being shift lead because I keep trading out of closing duties and I’m not dependable for those after-hours checks. And I was trying to work toward a permanent management position, so… yeah, it feels like this bat thing could actually mess that up. That’s probably what scares me most besides the bat itself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar reaction if you encountered another animal that you're not typically afraid of, in the same situation where you usually fear bats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not the same way. Like, if it was a bird or something in the back hallway, I’d probably jump because nobody likes something suddenly flying around near their head, you know? But with bats it’s different.\n\nI know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I’ve been told that, and I get it when I’m standing here talking about it. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the corner of my eye back there, my brain just goes straight to danger, like I’m in real trouble. It doesn’t really give me time to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you saw a large moth or another flying insect in the stockroom?\n\nPatient: A big moth… I mean, I’d probably hate it, yeah. Nobody wants some thing flapping around in a stockroom, especially with those awful lights back there—half the time they buzz anyway, which doesn’t help.\n\nBut no, I don’t think it would hit me the same as a bat. With bats, even if I know in my head it probably isn’t going to hurt me, the second I see something that looks like one—or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. Like it skips over the thinking part.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear is exclusively of bats or do you think you could be equally afraid of other animals if you encountered them in a similar situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s mostly bats. I mean, I don’t love animals loose in a store in general—one time we had a pigeon get near the front doors and everyone made a whole production out of it, customers taking videos and everything—but that didn’t do the same thing to me.\n\nWith bats, it’s like… I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. It just hits different than other animals.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.\n\nSo, I don’t know. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides bats or things that resemble bats, that can trigger a similar anxious reaction in you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, nothing really comes to mind the way the bat thing does. Like, I get stressed about normal stuff—customers being rude, schedules changing, my car making that noise again, which I keep meaning to get looked at—but that’s not the same kind of jolt.\n\nSo… besides bats or something that my brain thinks might be a bat, I haven’t really noticed anything else that sets me off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. Outside of that, I haven’t really noticed myself walking around anxious all the time.\n\nDoctor: Do you experience anxiety when you're not in a situation where you might encounter a bat or something that resembles one, such as when you're at home or with friends?\n\nPatient: At home? Um… I don’t think so, not really. Like, I can be annoyed or stressed about regular stuff—bills, dishes piling up, my neighbor’s dog barking at weird hours, that kind of thing—but I wouldn’t call it the same anxiety.\n\nWith friends, I’m usually okay too. Unless someone starts joking about bats, I guess, or tells some story and my mind goes there. But just sitting around or watching a game or whatever, no, I haven’t really noticed that same feeling.\n\nDoctor: Do you worry about having a panic attack when you're not thinking about bats?\n\nPatient: Um… worry about having one when I’m not thinking about bats? I don’t think so. I mean, I don’t usually sit there at home thinking, “what if I panic,” if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes after a long shift I’m kind of wound up, but that’s more like… my feet hurt, I’m replaying some annoying customer thing in my head, or wondering if I forgot to put something back on the shelf. But not really worrying a panic thing is going to happen out of nowhere. At least, I haven’t noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel anxious when you think about going to work, even if you're not thinking about bats at that moment?\n\nPatient: Going to work… I mean, sometimes I don’t want to go, but that’s more because it’s work, you know? Like if I know we’re short-staffed or there’s a sale and people are going to be lined up complaining about prices not ringing right.\n\nBut anxious like that? I don’t know. I haven’t really noticed it unless my mind starts wandering into that whole bat thing somehow. Most mornings I’m just thinking about coffee, traffic, whether my shirt’s clean… normal stuff, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety about bats is the main thing that's been bothering you, or are there other worries that also keep you up at night?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I mean… it’s mostly the bat thing, I think. I have normal worries, like anyone—work schedules, money stuff, whether my car’s making that noise again, you know, the usual junk that pops into your head when you’re trying to sleep.\n\nBut the thing that really gets under my skin is bats. Like, I know in my head if there was a bat in the store it probably isn’t going to hurt me. I know that. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter back in the hallway, my brain just… it reacts like I’m in real danger. Like before I’ve even had time to figure out what it actually was.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety about bats limited to when you're at work or can it happen anywhere?\n\nPatient: It can happen anywhere, I guess, if something makes me think it’s a bat. Work is where it’s been the most obvious, because of that back hallway and the stockroom and all those weird shadows—honestly the lighting back there is terrible, they keep saying they’ll fix it but, you know, retail.\n\nBut yeah, it’s not like the building itself is the magic thing. If I see something that looks like a bat, or even just a quick flutter out of the corner of my eye, my mind kind of jumps straight to danger before I can even think it through. I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially if it’s just in a store or whatever, but in the moment my body doesn’t seem to care what I know.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would feel anxious if you were in a situation where you thought you might encounter something else that you're afraid of, like spiders or snakes?\n\nPatient: I don’t know, honestly. I mean, everybody’s got things they don’t like, right? Like I’m not exactly volunteering to hold a snake or anything, but I can’t say I’ve really noticed the same kind of reaction.\n\nSpiders are… I mean, if one’s in the bathroom I’ll get a shoe or a cup, depending how big it is. My cousin used to keep one of those huge hairy spiders—tarantula, I think—and I never understood why someone would want that in their house, but that’s his business, I guess.\n\nSo, I don’t know. Maybe I’d be uncomfortable, but I haven’t really had that same “oh crap, I need to get out of here” thing unless it’s the bat stuff.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your anxiety would be triggered by something that isn't a bat but has some similarity to it, such as a bird or another flying creature?\n\nPatient: Yeah, maybe. I mean, if it’s just a bird outside, like sitting on a wire or whatever, that doesn’t usually bother me. My neighbor feeds them and then they’re all over the place, which is kind of annoying because they get on the cars, but that’s not the same thing.\n\nIt’s more like… if something moves like that, you know? A quick flutter, especially in the back hallway at work where the lights are bad, my brain just jumps to “bat” before I can sort it out. I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me, but if I see anything that looks like one, or it just flashes by for a second, my body treats it like I’m in real danger.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything else, besides bats or things that resemble bats, that can trigger a similar anxious reaction in you?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I mean, nothing really comes to mind the way the bat thing does. Like, I get stressed about normal stuff—customers being rude, schedules changing, my car making that noise again, which I keep meaning to get looked at—but that’s not the same kind of jolt.\n\nSo… besides bats or something that my brain thinks might be a bat, I haven’t really noticed anything else that sets me off like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you find yourself avoiding certain places or situations because you're afraid of encountering a bat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess I do, more than I’d like to admit. Like at work, I’ve been trying to swap out of closing shifts when I can, because that’s when I end up near the back areas and everything’s quieter and darker.\n\nAnd the loading area—there’s this one part by the dumpsters, and I’ll take the long way around it even though it’s stupid because it adds time. I tell people it’s just because I don’t feel like dealing with the carts back there or whatever, but really I’m thinking about bats.\n\nIf I have to check the stairwell, I still do it, but I kind of rush through it. I’ll have my phone in my hand with the flashlight ready, and I’m tense the whole time, like I’m waiting for something to fly at me. It’s embarrassing, honestly.\n\nDoctor: Are there any other areas of your life, outside of work, where you find yourself avoiding places or situations because of your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I mean, not as much, I don’t think. It’s mostly work because that’s where I’ve been doing all that stuff—trying to swap closing shifts, going the long way around the loading area, and then if I have to check the stairwell I just kind of make myself do it fast, phone in my hand, scared the whole time.\n\nAt home it’s not really the same setup, you know? Though now that you ask, I’m thinking about my garage light, it flickers sometimes and I really need to change the bulb, but I keep forgetting—well, maybe not forgetting, I just hate messing with it. But the main avoiding is definitely those work areas.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of bats affect your social life or leisure activities in any way?\n\nPatient: Social life… I don’t know, I’m not exactly out camping every weekend or anything. I mostly just go home, watch TV, maybe grab food with a buddy if schedules line up. My brother keeps trying to get me to go to this outdoor movie thing, but honestly that’s not really the big issue.\n\nThe bigger thing is work. Like, the closing stuff has become a problem. My manager’s started kind of questioning whether I can really handle being shift lead because I keep trading out of closing duties and I’m not dependable for those after-hours checks. And I was trying to work toward a permanent management position, so… yeah, it feels like this bat thing could actually mess that up. That’s probably what scares me most besides the bat itself, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have a similar reaction if you encountered another animal that you're not typically afraid of, in the same situation where you usually fear bats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so, not the same way. Like, if it was a bird or something in the back hallway, I’d probably jump because nobody likes something suddenly flying around near their head, you know? But with bats it’s different.\n\nI know, logically, a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I mean, I’ve been told that, and I get it when I’m standing here talking about it. But if I see something that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the corner of my eye back there, my brain just goes straight to danger, like I’m in real trouble. It doesn’t really give me time to think it through.\n\nDoctor: Would your reaction be the same if you saw a large moth or another flying insect in the stockroom?\n\nPatient: A big moth… I mean, I’d probably hate it, yeah. Nobody wants some thing flapping around in a stockroom, especially with those awful lights back there—half the time they buzz anyway, which doesn’t help.\n\nBut no, I don’t think it would hit me the same as a bat. With bats, even if I know in my head it probably isn’t going to hurt me, the second I see something that looks like one—or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway—my mind just treats it like I’m in real danger. Like it skips over the thinking part.\n\nDoctor: Would you say your fear is exclusively of bats or do you think you could be equally afraid of other animals if you encountered them in a similar situation?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I’d say it’s mostly bats. I mean, I don’t love animals loose in a store in general—one time we had a pigeon get near the front doors and everyone made a whole production out of it, customers taking videos and everything—but that didn’t do the same thing to me.\n\nWith bats, it’s like… I know a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I can say that right now and it makes sense. But if I see anything that looks like one, or even just a quick flutter in the back hallway, my mind treats it like I’m actually in danger. It just hits different than other animals.\n\nDoctor: Would you have a similar reaction to other flying animals, like birds, if you encountered them in the same situations that trigger your fear of bats?\n\nPatient: I mean, birds can startle me, sure. Like if one got loose in the store, I’d probably duck or swear or whatever—honestly the back hallway is so cramped with boxes sometimes that anything moving suddenly would make you jump.\n\nBut it’s not really the same. With bats, I know standing here that a bat in the store probably isn’t going to hurt me. I get that. But if I see something that looks like one, even just a quick flutter back there, my mind goes straight to like, “danger,” like I’m actually in real trouble. Birds don’t usually do that to me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Maybe not exactly the same? Like, if it was daytime in a park and everything was open, I’d probably tell myself, “Okay, it’s outside, it’s not trapped in here with me,” you know?

But honestly, if I saw it close enough, or if it did that quick fluttering thing, I think my brain would still jump to danger before I could talk myself down. It’s not really logical. Like, I know a bat probably isn’t going to hurt me, especially not just being there, but when I see something that looks like one, my body reacts like I’m in real trouble.

The store makes it worse, though—the back hallway, the stockroom, all those boxes and bad lighting. We’ve got this one fluorescent light that flickers near the break area, and every time maintenance says they fixed it, it starts again two weeks later. But yeah, even in a park, if it looked like a bat and moved suddenly, I don’t think I’d be calm.
